S tellar B lack H oles and C ompact S tellar R emnants
Guglielmo Costa, Martyna Chruślińska, Jakub Klencki, Floor S. Broekgaarden, Carl L. Rodriguez, Tana D. Joseph, Sara Saracino

Abstract
The recent observations of GW by the LVK ( LVK ) have provided a new opportunity for studying our Universe. By detecting several merging events of BH , LVK has spurred the astronomical community to improve theoretical models of single, binary, and multiple star evolution in order to better understand the formation of BBH ( BBH ) systems and interpret their observed properties. The final BBH system configuration before the merger depends on several processes, including those related to the evolution of the inner stellar structure and those due to the interaction with the companion and the environment (such as in stellar clusters). This Chapter provides a summary of the formation scenarios of stellar BH in single, binary, and multiple systems. We review all the important physical processes that affect the formation of BH and discuss the methodologies used to detect these elusive objects and constrain their properties.

Part I
Stellar evolution: single stars

Guglielmo Costa

I  Introduction

BH
are the densest and most extreme objects in the Universe. Their existence was hypothesised in 1916 by Schwarzschild
(
Schwarzschild 1916
)
as a solution to Einstein’s general relativity equations of space and time.
Theoretically, they can have any mass, and commonly, they are grouped into three families: stellar-mass
BH
, with a mass up to
100\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
,
IMBH
with mass in the range between
100
and
10^{6}\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, and
SMBH
with mass above
10^{6}\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
.
The recent detection of the first
GW
by coalescing stellar mass
BH

(
Abbott et al. 2016a
;
Abbott et al. 2016b
)
confirmed (once again) Einstein’s theory of relativity and the existence of
BH
. Moreover, it opens a new field of study, which uses
GW
for observing and studying the Universe.
During the last few years, the interferometers of the
LVK
have observed about 90 merger events
(
Abbott et al. 2021a
;
The LIGO Scientific Collaboration et al. 2021
)
, all with progenitor
BH
masses in the stellar mass range.
The formation scenarios of such kind of
BH
and
BBH
is still debated.
Although the general idea of single stellar mass
BH
’ formation is commonly accepted – that a
BH
could be created from the gravitational collapse of a massive star after the end of all nuclear-burning stages as postulated in 1939 by Oppenheimer, Volkoff, and Snyder
Oppenheimer and Snyder 1939
;
Oppenheimer and Volkoff 1939
– the details of the formation are still discussed.
Uncertainties in many physical processes may significantly change the standard picture of massive stars evolution, ultimately changing the predicted final outcome.
On top of that, the coupling of two stars (or
BH
) is needed to form a
BBH
that will merge due to
GW
emission. This adds a new layer of complexity to the problem.
Nowadays, the two main studied formation scenarios of
BBH
are the
isolated channel
– in which two stars are born together and evolve through all the binary evolutionary processes until they form a
BBH
system – and the
dynamical channel
– in which a population of stars born in a cluster, interact multiple times during the evolution leading to the formation of
BBH
.
Both scenarios help to interpret and understand the data obtained by the
GW
detectors.

In this first Part, we will focus on single stars and briefly review the evolution of massive stars, which can be quite complex.
After a short presentation of the main concepts of the stellar theory and stellar classification, we will introduce the main physical processes that affect the evolution of massive stars and their outcome, namely, stellar winds, mixing processes, and final explosive (or not) phases.

II  Overview on stellar evolution

Stars are self-gravitating objects composed of (almost) fully ionised gas in hydrostatic and thermal equilibrium.
Their evolution is regulated by the thermonuclear reactions that act in their cores.
The energy provided by the endothermic nuclear reactions is the fundamental ingredient in keeping them up against gravity and shining over time.
At the beginning of their life, stars are mainly composed of hydrogen (
68-75\%
) and helium (
24-30\%
), with a smaller part of heavier chemical elements (
\sim 0-2\%
), which astronomers commonly refer to as “metals”.
The metal content of stars in mass fraction, Z, is also called metallicity.
Usually, stellar models adopt a chemical composition scaled with the solar one, but its exact composition remains a matter of debate
(
Grevesse and Sauval 1998
;
Asplund et al. 2009
;
Caffau et al. 2011
;
Asplund et al. 2021
)
.
Although stellar evolution is quite a complex process, the nuclear evolution of the central regions could be simply described as a series of burning cycles
1
1

1

Here, we are using the astronomers’ terminology. Although we are talking about nuclear fusion processes, we refer to them as burning or combustion processes.
.
Each cycle can be divided into four steps, which are: (i) core contraction, (ii) core heating, (iii) nuclear burning, and (iv) fuel exhaustion
(
Kippenhahn et al. 2013
)
. At the end of each cycle, the star must readjust to a new equilibrium, and a new cycle (or evolutionary phase) begins.
During their lifetime, stars progressively burn chemical elements from the lighter ones (i.e. hydrogen) to the heavier ones.
Stars spend most of their life,
85-90\%
, burning hydrogen in their cores. This phase is called
MS
(
MS
), which is the locus in the observed luminosity versus surface temperature diagram in which stars are more abundant (see Section
II.3
).
The second longest phase is
CHeB
(
CHeB
), in which stars spend
10-15\%
of their lifetime
(
Maeder 2009
)
.
Depending on their initial mass (see Section
II.2
), stars may ignite and burn elements heavier than helium.
The advanced phases are much faster than the other two main burning phases, and the evolution from carbon-burning ignition to the final explosion (or collapse) is only
\sim 0.001
of the total lifetime. The stellar lifetime mainly depends on the star’s mass (see Section
II.2
), but also on the metallicity and initial angular momentum.

II.1
Historical background

The theory of stellar structure and evolution has been developed since the beginning of the twentieth century.
Pioneering work has been done by
Eddington 1918
, which set the ground for the theoretical understanding of stars.
Later, after the discovery of the tunnel effect by Gamov in 1928,
Atkinson and Houtermans 1929
first postulated that thermonuclear reactions in stellar cores could be an energy source for stars.
At the same time, the breakthrough works of
Payne 1925
and
Russell 1929
suggested that hydrogen is the most abundant element in the Sun and other stars.
Approximately ten years later,
Bethe and Critchfield 1938
and
Bethe 1939
described the
proton-proton
chain and the carbon-nitrogen-oxygen (
CNO
) cycle, which are the two main channels for the formation of helium from hydrogen.
The proton-proton chain consists of a series of consecutive proton captures.
The first part leads to the formation of
3
He; then, the chain can continue through three different branches (namely
pp1
,
pp2
,
pp3
). pp1 is initially the most frequent chain, transforming six protons into one helium plus two protons. Later, at higher temperatures, pp2 and pp3 increase their relative importance.
Both convert one
7
Be plus a proton into two
4
He atoms.
The CNO cycle becomes dominant over the proton-proton chain for temperatures above
1.5\times 10^{7}
K. It requires the presence of some C, N, or O elements that act as catalysts for the formation of helium.

In 1952,
Salpeter 1952
described the main reactions of
CHeB
. During this phase, stars convert helium into carbon through the
triple-
\alpha
reaction
2
2

2

An
\alpha
particle is a
4
He nucleus. The triple-
\alpha
reaction requires temperatures above
10^{8}
K and proceeds in two steps, since the triple encounter is very improbable. The first is
4
He +
4
He
\rightarrow

8
Be, and the second is
8
Be +
4
He
\rightarrow

12
C +
\gamma
.
, then carbon to oxygen with
12
C +
4
He
\rightarrow

16
O +
\gamma
and finally oxygen to neon with
16
O +
4
He
\rightarrow

20
Ne +
\gamma
.
For a detailed description of nuclear reaction rates in stars, we refer the reader to
Iliadis 2007
.

The stellar theory received a great boost in the 1950s and 1960s, thanks to the arrival of the first computers and the development of computational sciences.
In the late 1950s, the first models of the Sun appeared in the literature
(
Schwarzschild et al. 1957
)
.
During the second half of the 1960s, innovative numerical methods allowed astronomers to solve stellar equations in a fast and reliable fashion, and the first simulations of the evolution of stars during the main evolutionary phases appeared
(
Henyey et al. 1964
;
Kippenhahn et al. 1967
)
.
More details on stellar structure and numerical methodologies can be found in
Maeder 2009
;
Kippenhahn et al. 2013
.

After those first important steps, stellar evolution theory has been improved thanks to more detailed treatments and careful calibrations of stellar processes, such as stellar winds, internal mixing, advanced burning stages, rotation, and magnetic fields, to name a few.

Nowadays, it is possible to model stars with a wide range of initial masses (between
\sim 0.08\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
and
\sim 1000\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, where
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}=1.989\times 10^{33}\,
g is the mass of the Sun) and compositions (from almost metal-free stars to supra-solar abundances, see Section
III
).
Stellar evolution theory is useful not only for studying stars themselves but also because it is the backbone of many other astrophysical fields
(
Clayton 1983
)
.
For instance, stellar models are used to study the ages and properties of star clusters
(
Gossage et al. 2018
;
Goudfrooij et al. 2018
;
Costa et al. 2019a
;
Szécsi and Wünsch 2019
;
Milone and Marino 2022
;
Milone et al. 2023
)
, to understand galaxies and Milky Way formation and evolution
(
Gatto et al. 2017
;
Rosdahl et al. 2018
;
Valentini et al. 2019
;
Dal Tio et al. 2022
)
, supernova, gamma-ray burst progenitors and chemical yields
(
Yoon et al. 2006a
;
Sukhbold et al. 2016
;
Limongi and Chieffi 2018
;
Aguilera-Dena et al. 2020
)
, obtain host-star parameters for exo-planets
(
Gallet et al. 2017
;
Maldonado et al. 2019
;
Maldonado et al. 2020
)
, and derive the
CO
(
CO
) mass spectrum of single and binary populations
(
Spera and Mapelli 2017
;
Kruckow et al. 2018a
;
Stevenson et al. 2019
;
Mapelli et al. 2020a
;
Woosley and Heger 2021a
;
Iorio et al. 2023
;
Fragos et al. 2023
)
to tackle the open problems raised by the new observed gravitational waves events
(
Abbott et al. 2016a
;
Abbott et al. 2020a
;
Abbott et al. 2020b
)
.

II.2
Mass ranges and stellar remnants

From a purely theoretical point of view, the evolution of a star could be uniquely determined by just three parameters:
the initial mass, the chemical composition, and the angular momentum.
Without worrying about metallicity and rotation, stars’ evolution and final fate are mainly determined by their initial mass at the first order of approximation.

Figure
1
:
This figure shows the stars’ classification depending on the initial mass (
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}
).
Different colours indicate the final remnant of the evolution.

\tau_{\mathrm{MS}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{CHeB}}
are the lifetimes of the
MS
and
CHeB
, respectively.
\tau_{\mathrm{end}}
is the timescale of the last phases, i.e. from the end of the
CHeB
to the formation of the
CO
.
The transition masses and the timescales can change for different metallicities. The limits for very low-mass, low-mass, and intermediate-mass stars are taken from
Nguyen et al. 2022
. Transition mass between the formation of
NS
and
BH
is taken from
Fryer et al. 2012
. Limits of the no remnant zone are taken from
Spera and Mapelli 2017
, with
Z=2\times 10^{-4}
.

At the low end of the mass range (
\lesssim 0.07-0.08\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
), there are Brown dwarfs, i.e. sub-stellar objects that do not reach the conditions for burning hydrogen stably. Such objects burn deuterium in the early stages and, later, are maintained by the internal heat content
(
Chabrier and Baraffe 2000
)
.
Stars with initial masses from
\sim 0.08\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
to
\sim 0.8\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
are called very low-mass stars.
They are massive enough to reach the thermal equilibrium and stably burn hydrogen.
The
MS
lifetime of these stars is longer than the age of the universe.
After
MS
, the helium core contracts until gravity is balanced by the degeneracy pressure of the electrons. Finally, they die as helium
WD
after the ejection of the envelope.
In the mass range from
\sim 0.8\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
to
\sim 2\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, there are the low-mass stars.
They are massive enough to ignite helium under strongly degenerate conditions (usually referred to as He-flash).
After the end of
CHeB
, the carbon-oxygen core begins to contract, and the electrons become degenerate. Ultimately, these stars die as carbon-oxygen
WD

(
Chantereau et al. 2015
)
.
Very low-mass and low-mass stars (with an initial mass
\lesssim 2\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
) are the most common stars in the universe
(
Salpeter 1955
;
Kroupa 2001a
)
.
These stars may live from a few to hundreds of Gyr in the
MS
phase.
Low-mass stars can be divided into sub-groups depending on their structure. Stars with a mass below
\sim 1

\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
have radiative cores and convective envelopes (like our Sun). On the other hand, low-mass stars with a mass greater than
\sim 1

\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
already develop a convective core and a radiative envelope
(
Nguyen et al. 2022
)
.

Stars in the mass range between
\sim 2

\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
and
\sim 8

\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
are called intermediate-mass stars. They are massive enough to ignite helium in non-degenerate cores (i.e. avoiding the He-flash).
These stars may live from about 0.1 to a few Gyrs. During the hydrogen-burning phase, they have a well-formed convective core and a radiative envelope.
After the
CHeB
, both low-mass and intermediate-mass stars evolve by contracting their cores. The contraction continues until the pressure by electron degeneracy is high enough to sustain the core. These stars are not massive enough to ignite carbon in their cores and, over time, winds completely peel off the stellar envelope, exposing the degenerate core. Such stars produce planetary nebulae and end their lives as
WD
.

Between about 8
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
and 10
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, stars are massive enough to ignite carbon in their cores. The final fate of such stars depends on the mass of the neon-oxygen-magnesium core after the carbon-burning phase. If the core mass remains below the critical limit of
\sim 1.37\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}

(
Nomoto 1984
)
, stars will end their life as neon-oxygen
WD
.
Electron capture reactions lead to core collapse if the core mass grows to
1.37\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
(due to the burning of the helium in the shell above the core).
Such stars end their life with an electron-capture supernova, and a low mass
NS
is the outcome of the evolution (see Section
III.4
for more details).
If the star builds a core more massive than
\sim 1.37\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, it ignites neon and proceeds through the advanced burning phases, like massive stars (see below).
Such stars will end their lives as
NS
.
These limits for intermediate-mass stars depend on the stellar properties (metallicity, rotation, extra mixing, etc.) and on the numerical details of the models
(
Siess 2007
;
Poelarends et al. 2008
;
Kippenhahn et al. 2013
)
.

Stars with masses greater than about 10
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
are called massive stars.
They are the most luminous and hottest stars in the Universe and the shortest-lived (from hundreds to a few million years).
After the core carbon burning, these stars sequentially proceed to advanced burning stages in non-degenerate conditions (neon-, oxygen- and silicon-burning) up to the formation of iron-group elements.
Their final configuration (usually referred to as the onion skin model) consists of a series of shells composed of heavier elements going from the surface toward the centre, in which there is the iron core.
Massive stars end their life with a
CC
(
CC
) supernova event or with a direct collapse without supernova explosion (see Section
III.4
for more details).
In the first approximation, massive stars with an initial mass below
\sim
20 - 25
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
end their life as
NS
, while those above
\sim 25\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
form
BH
.
Very massive stars, with an initial mass greater than about 110
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, could leave no remnant if they enter the
PI
(
PI
) regime.
PI
could trigger an explosive ignition of oxygen that can completely destroy the star, ultimately resulting in a powerful explosion known as
PISN
(
PISN
)
(
Woosley 2017
)
.
From the theoretical point of view, the upper mass that a star can have is still a debated problem. However, observations of the R136 stellar cluster in the Large Magellanic Cloud have found stars with a mass of
\sim 300\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}

(
Crowther et al. 2010a
)
.

Figure
1
shows a summary of the stars’ classification presented so far. It is worth stressing again that such mass limits do not depend solely on the initial mass. Other factors, such as stellar metallicity and rotation, can play a role.
Moreover, the theoretical uncertainties on the stellar processes, such as internal mixing and stellar winds, still make these limits quite uncertain, particularly for massive stars, where small changes in the input physics can significantly impact their stellar properties, evolution, and final fate.
Multiple studies have demonstrated that different models of massive stars, based on varying physical processes like mixing, mass loss rates, and rotation, can lead to very different evolutions and outcomes
(
Martins and Palacios 2013
;
Jones et al. 2015
;
Agrawal et al. 2020
, see)
.

II.3
The HR diagram

The
HR
(
HR
) diagram is one of the main tools astronomers use to characterise stars.
It shows the relation between the stellar effective temperature (
T_{eff}
, i.e. the temperature at the stellar photosphere) and the luminosity
3
3

3

The luminosity is the total amount of radiation emitted per unit of time.
(
L
) of stars.
The effective temperature is strictly related to the luminosity and the radius (
R
) of the star through the following relation:

L=4\pi R^{2}\sigma T_{eff}^{4},

(II.1)

where
\sigma
is the Stefan-Boltzmann constant of radiation.

The diagram is named after
Hertzsprung 1911
and
Russell 1914
, who discovered that stars do not randomly scatter along this diagram but fall into several groups.
This remarkable discovery strongly affected the development of modern astronomy and astrophysics.
The
HR
diagram is also referred to as the “colour-magnitude diagram” since the stellar colour (or spectral type) is related to
T_{eff}
(stars look blue for
T_{eff}>10^{4}
K, yellow for
10^{4}<T_{eff}<5\times 10^{3}
K or red for
T_{eff}<5\times 10^{3}
K), and the absolute magnitude is related to the luminosity.
In this Part, we call the
HR
diagram the plot of luminosity versus effective temperature.

The most striking feature of the
HR
diagram is that the position of stars (i.e. stars’ luminosity and effective temperature) depends on their evolutionary stage.
Most of the stars that we observe lie on the
MS
, a narrow diagonal band in the diagram in which they are converting hydrogen into helium. The locus on the diagram where stars ignite thermonuclear reactions and enter
MS
is called
ZAMS
(
ZAMS
).
This is assumed as the beginning of evolution, and the mass in this stage (
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}
) is the initial mass.

Figure
2
shows the position of the
ZAMS
in the diagram for stellar models computed with different initial masses. At the
ZAMS
, stars are almost totally homogeneous, and their luminosity is proportional to their mass (
L\propto{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}^{3.5}
).

Stars with a mass
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\lesssim 0.75\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
barely evolve in the
HR
diagram, and they remain near the
ZAMS
for a time larger than the age of the Universe (the Hubble time).
Stars with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\gtrsim 0.8\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
are massive enough to finish the
MS
phase before one Hubble time.
When hydrogen is depleted, the core contracts and the star’s envelope expands at almost constant luminosity. In the
HR
diagram, the star moves toward lower temperatures (because of the relation shown in Equation
II.1
) and becomes a
RG
(
RG
).
The locus where
RG
stars lie in the
HR
diagram is called
RGB
(
RGB
). In this phase, stars burn hydrogen in a shell above the helium core and start becoming more and more luminous (ascending the branch).
During the
RGB
phase, stars develop a deep convective envelope which dredges up the hydrogen-burning products (mainly nitrogen, but also isotopes of carbon and oxygen), which enrich the stellar surface (this process is called
first dredge-up
).

Figure
2
:
The plot shows the
HR
diagram of a set of tracks with
Z=0.014
computed with
parsec
stellar evolutionary code
(
Costa et al. 2021
;
Nguyen et al. 2022
)
. The ”plasma” colour map indicates the
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}
of very low- and low-mass (VLM + LM) stars. The ”viridis” map indicates the intermediate-mass (IM) stars, while the ”cividis” map indicates the massive and very massive (M + VM) stars. The dashed diagonal black line shows the star’s location at the
ZAMS
. The small red stars show the final position of the star at the end of the evolution (for stars with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}>2\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
), at the tip of the
RGB
(for stars with
0.7<{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}/\,\rm{M}_{\odot}<2
), or after 30 Gyr (for stars with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}<0.70\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
). The
CHeB
(or horizontal-branch) phase of stars with
0.7<{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}/\,\rm{M}_{\odot}<2
is not shown for clarity purposes.

After the
RGB
phase, stars massive enough ignite helium and enter the
CHeB
phase.
The new energy source provided by the nuclear burning of helium leads the core to expand and the envelope to contract. The effective temperature increases again, and the star moves to the bluer part of the
HR
diagram.
During the
CHeB
phase, intermediate-mass and massive stars may draw a loop in the
HR
diagram, usually referred to as the
blue loop
4
4

4

The blue loop may cross an instability strip, in which stars begin to pulsate as classical Cepheids
(
Chiosi et al. 1993
, see)
. These stars are important cosmic distance indicators due to the correlation between pulsation period and luminosity
(
Freedman et al. 2001
;
Riess et al. 2021
, see)
.
.
After
CHeB
, the core contracts, and the envelope expands again and moves toward the red part of the
HR
diagram again.

Low-mass and intermediate-mass stars move to the
AGB
(
AGB
) phase.

AGB
stars move to the reddest and most luminous part of the diagram, running near and almost parallel to the Hayashi line
5
5

5

The Hayashi line is the locus of fully convective stars in the
HR
diagram
(
Kippenhahn et al. 2013
)
. It is located at very low temperatures and defines a forbidden region (on its right) in the
HR
diagram for the stars.

(
Hayashi 1961
)
.
In the early-
AGB
phase, stars burn helium in a shell above the carbon-oxygen core and undergo another dredge-up episode (called
second dredge-up
).
After this stage, stars enter the
AGB
phase, which is characterised by two burning shells above the carbon-oxygen core (H- and He- shell burning at the same time).
The double shell burning phase is thermally unstable and leads to a cyclic series of thermal pulses, which increase and decrease the star’s radius, luminosity and effective temperature.
This phase is called
TP-AGB
(
TP-AGB
).
After each pulse, the convective envelope extends deeper into the star and eventually extracts products of the He-shell burning, which enrich the stellar surface (a process known as
third dredge-up
).

AGB
stars suffer from powerful winds driven by dust and are one of the polluters which enrich the interstellar medium with heavy elements.

Stars with
8\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}\lesssim{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\lesssim 10\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
evolve similarly to the most massive intermediate-mass stars. In the advanced phases, they move to the
AGB
, but being more luminous, are labelled as super-
AGB
stars
(
Ritossa et al. 1996
)
.
They can ignite carbon in their core and die as
WD
or
NS
depending on the oxygen-neon-magnesium core mass (see Section
II.2
and
III.4
).

Massive and very massive stars (with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\gtrsim 10\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
) may have a very different evolution and fate, depending on their initial properties, and the physics adopted
(
Vanbeveren et al. 1998
;
Langer 2012
;
Meynet et al. 2015
)
.
Stars with a mass
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\lesssim 35\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
evolve similarly to most massive intermediate-mass stars, with the difference that after the
CHeB
, they move to lower temperatures, becoming
RSG
(
RSG
).
After carbon ignition, the evolution becomes so fast that stars no longer move in the
HR
diagram and explode as
RSG
.
Although the core evolution is similar, stars with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\gtrsim 35\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
may end their lives differently depending on the metallicity.
This is because the main driver of the evolution of such stars is the mass loss via stellar winds. Winds may be strong due to the high luminosity of such stars, but they also are very sensitive to the stellar metal content (see Section
III.2
).
Metal-poor massive stars (Z
\leq 0.001
) do not experience strong winds during their life (they retain most of their envelope), and after the
CHeB
phase, they evolve and explode as
RSG
stars.
On the other hand, massive stars with a higher metal content (Z
>0.005
) may lose the largest part of their H-rich envelope during the
MS
and
CHeB
phases.
Such stripped stars move toward the hotter (blue) part of the
HR
diagram, becoming
WR
(
WR
) stars
6
6

6

WR
are named after
Wolf and Rayet 1867
, who discovered a few stars in the Cygnus constellation with the peculiar broad emission lines.
.

WR
are stars with stellar surfaces impoverished in hydrogen.
From the observational point of view, they are divided into several groups depending on their spectrum.
The two main groups are the WN stars which show the H-burning products (mainly helium and nitrogen) at their surface, and the WC stars, which show the He-burning products (carbon and oxygen).

WR
stars are considered a possible final evolutionary stage of massive stars, i.e. the Conti scenario
(
Conti 1975
;
Abbott and Conti 1987
;
Maeder and Conti 1994
;
Crowther 2007
;
Aadland et al. 2022
)
.
These stars are very luminous, hot and compact in radius. Their relatively small radius could play a role in the binary evolution (
WR
could avoid episodes of mass transfer at variance with the very expanded
RSG
), but also in the final
CC
explosion
(
Fernández et al. 2018a
, see Section
III.4
,)
. Figure
2
shows the position of massive stars at the end of the oxygen-burning phase (red stars) for models with an initial metallicity of Z = 0.014 and no rotation.

Observations of Galactic massive stars revealed the existence of a ‘forbidden’ region in which few stars are observed in the
HR
diagram, the so-called Humphreys-Davidson limit
(
Humphreys and Davidson 1994
)
.
Theoretically, stars near this region are expected to approach the Eddington luminosity
7
7

7

The Eddington limit is the luminosity at which radiation pressure balances the star’s surface gravity of the star
(
Eddington 1926
)
.
Generally, the ratio of radiative luminosity to the Eddington luminosity, i.e. the Eddington factor, is defined as
\Gamma_{E}=L\kappa_{\mathrm{e}}/4\pi cGM
, where
L
is the stellar luminosity,
\kappa_{\mathrm{e}}
is the electron scattering opacity,
c
is the speed of light,
G
is the gravity constant, and
M
is the stellar mass. When the star is near the limit,
\Gamma_{E}
approaches 1.
and experiences powerful winds (see Section
III.2
for more details).

III  Main physical processes that affect life and death of massive stars

Massive stars play a pivotal role in the history of the Universe.
They are principal actors in the galaxies’ chemical and spectral evolution due to their strong radiation and chemical nucleosynthesis footprint.
They are the progenitors of powerful supernovæ,
NS
and
BH
.
Moreover, massive stars enrich and perturb the interstellar medium with their strong winds and supernova ejections, possibly driving (or quenching) new star formation episodes.

The life of massive stars is shorter than that of low-mass and intermediate-mass stars (as seen in Section
II.2
).
Therefore, they are much rarer, and stellar models become even more important to study and understand the physics at play.
Although state-of-the-art models of massive stars have reached a very high level of detail, there is still a significant degree of uncertainty regarding their evolutionary path and final fate.
In particular, attention must be paid to every proposed relation between the initial mass (
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}
) and the final
CO
mass (
M_{\mathrm{rem}}
), because tiny changes in the adopted physics may lead to big differences at the end of the evolution.
The main processes that affect massive stars’ evolution are convection, stellar winds, rotation, magnetic fields, and the physics of the final explosion. These processes are reviewed in the following Section.

The stellar initial composition (i.e. the metallicity) also plays a role, but it is mostly related to the stellar winds (see Section
III.2
).
Generally, at
ZAMS
, stars with a low metal content are more compact than their metal-rich counterparts (mainly because of their lower opacity in the envelope), and they build slightly larger cores at the end of the
MS
and
CHeB
phases.
The first stars in the Universe (namely Population III or Pop III) are particularly interesting. They are almost metal-free stars (
Z\sim 0
), enriched only by the Big Bang nucleosynthesis.
At variance with more metal-rich stars, simulations suggest that Pop III stars form with a top-heavy initial mass function, predicting that massive Pop III stars were not rare in the early Universe
(
Bromm 2013
)
.
The predicted high number of massive stars in the early Universe, in addition to their properties – during the
MS
, they are strong emitters of UV radiation, because very hot and luminous – elect them between the major actors in the reionisation of the Universe
(
Schaerer 2002
;
Bromm 2013
;
Klessen and Glover 2023
)
.
Moreover, various studies suggest that Pop III stars may be the progenitors of the
BBH
merger events that we observe today
(
Liu and Bromm 2020
;
Costa et al. 2021
;
Farrell et al. 2021
;
Tanikawa et al. 2021a
;
Kinugawa et al. 2021a
;
Costa et al. 2023
;
Santoliquido et al. 2023
)
.

It is worth recalling that most massive stars live in binary
(
Sana et al. 2012a
)
or even in multiple systems
(
Moe and Di
Stefano 2017a
)
. Binary evolutionary processes may have a strong impact on the lives of massive stars and their final fate
(
de Mink et al. 2009
;
Langer 2012
;
Marchant et al. 2016a
;
Eldridge et al. 2017
;
Eldridge and Stanway 2022
)
. Therefore, treating them as individual stars might give a partial picture.
Binary processes, such as Roche lobe overflow, common envelope or even stellar collision/merger, could form peculiar stellar objects, such as stripped stars or “puffy” stars.
The former has a higher core-to-envelope mass ratio than standard stars with the same initial conditions and at the same evolutionary phases. The latter has a core-to-envelope mass ratio lower than standard stars (i.e., smaller cores and oversized envelopes).
Such peculiar stars have a different evolutionary path and final fate than their standard counterparts
(
Klencki et al. 2020
;
Renzo et al. 2020
;
Laplace et al. 2021
;
Ballone et al. 2022
;
Costa et al. 2022
)
. Binary processes and their impact on the final
BH
masses are reviewed in Part
II
of this book.

III.1
Convection and overshooting

Convection is a physical process that transports the excess energy from an inner (hotter) region to an outer (cooler) region in stars.
When a region becomes thermally unstable
8
8

8

A region of a star is considered thermally stable if a perturbed element (eddy or bubble) of material experiences a restoring force that brings it back to the starting position. On the contrary, a region is said to be unstable if the perturbed element experiences a force which drives it away from the starting position.
, convection arises, and large eddies of material rise and sink, mixing chemical elements and efficiently transporting energy.
In the cores of massive stars, convection provides fresh fuel to the active burning layers and brings the ashes of nuclear reactions outward.
It is a key process that regulates the lifetimes of each burning phase, affecting the final pre-supernova structure and explosion likelihood

(see
Langer 2012
;
Sukhbold and Woosley 2014
;
Schootemeijer et al. 2019
;
Kaiser et al. 2020
, and references therein)
.
Convection is still one of the open problems of stellar physics, and its treatment in stellar simulations is still under debate
(
Renzini 1987
;
Zahn 1991
;
Langer 2012
;
Salaris and Cassisi 2017
;
Kaiser et al. 2020
;
Andrassy et al. 2022
)
.

In stellar models, it is difficult to achieve a comprehensive treatment of convection. It is a very complex physical process that requires full 3-D hydrodynamical simulations to be addressed.
In the past decades, several steps forward have been done with 2-D and 3-D hydrodynamical simulations
(
Freytag et al. 1996
;
Herwig et al. 2006
;
Meakin and Arnett 2007
;
Magic et al. 2013
;
Arnett et al. 2018
;
Arnett et al. 2019
;
Andrassy et al. 2022
;
Rizzuti et al. 2022
, e.g.)
, but such models are still limited in the time and space domains to be consistently applied in stellar evolutionary models.
Typical timescales of stellar evolution are several orders of magnitude larger than those resolved by 3-D simulations
(
Cristini et al. 2017
, e.g.)
.

The most widely used theory to treat convective zones in 1-D stellar evolutionary codes is the
MLT
(
MLT
)
(
Böhm-Vitense 1958
)
.

MLT
provides an approximate methodology for treating convection locally and in 1-D, assuming hydrostatic equilibrium, constant energy flux, and local thermodynamic equilibrium. There are more refined theories for treating convection
(
Roxburgh 1978
;
Canuto and Mazzitelli 1991
, such as)
, but they are less common in stellar computations.
The
MLT
framework is characterised by the mixing length distance,
l_{\mathrm{MLT}}
, which is the distance covered by the convective bubble before it dissolves into its surroundings.
It is defined as
l_{\mathrm{MLT}}=\alpha_{\mathrm{MLT}}H_{\mathrm{P}}
, where
\alpha_{\mathrm{MLT}}
is the efficiency parameter, and
H_{\mathrm{P}}
is the pressure scale height.
In stellar models, the
\alpha_{\mathrm{MLT}}
parameter is usually calibrated to match the Sun’s luminosity, radius, and age, and its value may range between 1 and 2.5
(
Eggenberger et al. 2008
;
Bressan et al. 2012
;
Magic et al. 2015
)
.
Generally, once calibrated,
\alpha_{\mathrm{MLT}}
is assumed to be constant for all masses and not dependent on other quantities, such as metallicity.
Whether
\alpha_{\mathrm{MLT}}
may vary with stellar parameters is still an open debate; some authors suggest that it may depend on the effective temperature or metallicity
(
Magic et al. 2015
;
Joyce and Chaboyer 2018
;
Viani et al. 2018
, e.g.)
.
However, its calibration from stars other than Sun could be biased since the
\alpha_{\mathrm{MLT}}
uncertainties are also correlated to uncertainties from other sources, such as the stellar surface boundary condition
(
Choi et al. 2018
, see)
.

The success of
MLT
in describing the unstable regions is due to its simple implementation and good empirical agreement with the observations once properly calibrated.
In 1-D stellar models,
MLT
must be coupled with a criterion to define the convectively unstable borders and a prescription to include the convective boundary mixing, also known as overshooting. We discuss both in the following.
For further details on convection and convection treatment in stellar models, we suggest
Weiss et al. 2004
,
Maeder 2009
, and
Kippenhahn et al. 2013
.

III.1.1
Defining the convective borders

Convection may arise in different stellar regions and at different evolutionary phases, depending on the local physical condition of the matter.
The two most used prescriptions to define the boundaries of the unstable region are the
Schwarzschild

(
Schwarzschild 1958
)
and
Ledoux

(
Ledoux 1947
)
criteria.
The former criterion states that the region is stable if

\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}<\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}},

(III.1)

where
\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}
is the temperature gradient in the case of stable radiative energy transport, while
\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}
is the adiabatic temperature gradient for the unstable case.

The
Ledoux
criterion states that matter is stable when

\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}<\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}+\frac{\varphi}{\delta}\nabla_{\mu},

(III.2)

where
\varphi=\left(\partial\ln\rho/\partial\ln\mu\right)_{P,T}
and
\delta=-\left(\partial\ln\rho/\partial\ln T\right)_{P,\mu}

are thermodynamical derivatives and
\nabla_{\mu}=\partial\ln\mu/\partial\ln P
is the molecular weight gradient. For a perfect gas,
\varphi=\delta=1
.
In the case of a chemical homogeneous region,
\nabla_{\mu}=0
, the two criteria are identical.
The two temperature gradients are defined as follows

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{16\pi acG}\frac{\kappa LP}{MT^{4}}

(III.3)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}

\displaystyle=\frac{P\delta}{C_{P}\rho T},

(III.4)

where
a
is the radiation constant,
c
is the speed of light,
G
the gravitational constant. The other variables are the local thermodynamical quantities of a shell which encloses a mass
M
, where
\kappa
is the opacity,
L
is the luminosity,
P
the pressure,
T
the temperature,
\rho
the density,
C_{P}
is the specific heat at constant pressure.

Figure
3
:
This plot shows the main stability criteria for stellar models (assuming a perfect gas).
The red vertical line indicates the
Schwarzschild
one, while the blue line indicates the
Ledoux
one.
Coloured areas indicate the expected mixing processes. The pink area indicates the region where the material is stable, and no mixing occurs. The blue area indicates the region where the material is unstable, and convection occurs.
The green area indicates where semiconvection occurs. The magenta indicates where thermohaline mixing can occur.
Figure adapted from
(
Karakas and Lattanzio 2014
)

.

Figure
3
shows a way to visualise various mixing processes that may occur in stars.
The regions of the star in which
\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}>\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}+\frac{\varphi}{\delta}\nabla_{\mu}
, are fully convective (upper right part of the figure), while regions which satisfy the
Schwarzschild
criterion (i.e.,
\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}<\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}
) are stable, and the energy is transported radiatively (lower left part of the figure).
When matter is stable according to the
Ledoux
criterion but unstable according to the
Schwarzschild
criterion (i.e.,
\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}<\nabla_{\mathrm{rad}}<\nabla_{\mathrm{ad}}+\frac{\varphi}{\delta}\nabla_{\mu}
), semi-convection instability occurs (green lower middle part of the figure).
Semi-convection may occur during the
MS
of massive stars, in which the core progressively retreats, leaving a chemical profile above it.
In such regions, the radiative gradient increases and becomes larger than the adiabatic one, but the positive molecular gradient (
\nabla_{\mu}>0
, i.e.
\mu
increases toward the centre) stabilises the layer.
That layer becomes vibrationally unstable, and the plasma starts to oscillate around an equilibrium point, producing slow mixing
(
Schwarzschild and Härm 1958
;
Kato 1966
;
Langer 1991
;
Garaud 2018
;
Anders et al. 2022a
)
.
In 1-D stellar models, semi-convection can be treated as a diffusive process
(
Langer et al. 1985
)
, with a typical timescale similar to the thermal one, which is longer than the convection timescale but shorter than the nuclear one.
In long phases such as
MS
, mixing by semiconvection does not affect the evolution much, and models computed with the
Ledoux
criterion are similar to those computed with the
Schwarzschild
one
(
Langer et al. 1985
)
.
Studies of convection with 3-D simulations show that the semi-convection oscillations produce a mixing which moves the unstable boundary to the one defined by the
Schwarzschild
criterion
(
Kato 1966
;
Arnett et al. 2019
;
Anders et al. 2022a
)
.
On the other hand, semi-convection could play a role in post-
MS
phases, affecting, for instance, the helium core size at the end of
CHeB

(see
Chiosi and Maeder 1986
;
Langer 2012
, and references therein)
. However, the possible interplay of semi-convection with other processes, such as overshooting, remains a matter of discussion
(
Kippenhahn et al. 2013
)
.

In regions where matter is stable according to the
Schwarzschild
criterion, but there is a
\mu
inversion (when
\nabla_{\mu}<0
), thermohaline mixing occurs (upper left part of the figure).

Ulrich 1972
developed a theory of thermohaline mixing, which was then generalised by
Kippenhahn et al. 1980
. Later,
Eggleton et al. 2006
stressed the importance of thermohaline mixing in RGB, discovering that the hydrogen-burning shell can generate a
\mu
inversion that can trigger the mixing
(see also
Charbonnel and Zahn 2007
;
Karakas and Lattanzio 2014
, for further details)
.
Thermohaline mixing could be an important process to be included in binary evolutionary models if the accretor star gains helium-rich matter from the donor star
(
Wellstein et al. 2001a
)
.

To conclude, there is still an open debate in the stellar community on what criterion should be used.
In the literature, there exist sets of stellar models computed either with the
Schwarzschild
criterion
(
Ekström et al. 2012
;
Ritter et al. 2018
;
Hidalgo et al. 2018
;
Amard et al. 2019
;
Nguyen et al. 2022
, e.g.)
, or with the
Ledoux
one
(
Heger et al. 2000a
;
Brott et al. 2011a
;
Choi et al. 2016
;
Limongi and Chieffi 2018
;
Szécsi et al. 2022
, e.g.)
.

III.1.2
Convective boundary mixing or overshooting

Several studies have found that the mixing provided only by the convectively unstable regions was insufficient to reproduce stellar observations, and an additional source of mixing was needed beyond the convective limit
(
Maeder 1975
;
Bressan et al. 1981
;
Zahn 1991
;
Rosenfield et al. 2017
;
Claret and Torres 2019
;
Aerts 2021
, e.g.)
. This led to the concept of overshooting
(
Zahn 1991
, see)
.
3-D simulations show that this type of extra mixing, called convective boundary mixing
(
Denissenkov et al. 2013
)
, is generated from the interplay of different processes
(
Arnett et al. 2019
, e.g.)
.

In the 1-D framework of the
MLT
, only the radial velocity is considered, and the main idea behind the overshooting process is the following. When a rising (sinking) bubble reaches the convective border, the acceleration is zero, but the velocity is not.
Therefore, the bubble overshoots the convective border due to its inertia and enters the stable radiative region, in which the restoring forces brake and stop the bubble.
This process mixes matter between the convective and radiative regions.
The core overshooting process affects several properties of stars, such as lifetime, luminosity, and core mass. It affects not only the
MS
but also post-
MS
burning phases
(
Meakin and Arnett 2007
;
Denissenkov et al. 2013
;
Cristini et al. 2017
;
Rizzuti et al. 2022
)
.

In 1-D stellar evolutionary models, overshooting is implemented in different ways, and the most common are described below.
The first method is the so-called
penetrative convection
scheme
(
Saslaw and Schwarzschild 1965
;
Shaviv and Salpeter 1973
;
Zahn 1991
)
, in which the convective border is moved by an overshooting distance,

d_{\mathrm{ov}}=\lambda_{\mathrm{ov}}H_{\mathrm{P}}
, where
\lambda_{\mathrm{ov}}
is the overshooting parameter. The overshooting region is then treated as unstable, and the chemical elements are fully mixed.
Some authors compute
d_{\mathrm{ov}}
by integrating the path of convective bubbles across the unstable border
(
Maeder 1975
;
Bressan et al. 1981
;
Zahn 1991
, called the ballistic approach, )
.

A second methodology is the exponential decaying overshooting in the diffusive scheme
(
Herwig et al. 1997
)
. Extra mixing is applied in the region above the convective border, with a diffusion coefficient (
D_{\mathrm{ov}}
) that exponentially decays. It is defined as

D_{\mathrm{ov}}=D_{\mathrm{0}}\exp{ \left( 2\frac{r - r_0}{f_\mathrm{ov} H_\mathrm{P}}\right)},

(III.5)

where
D_{\mathrm{0}}
is the diffusion coefficient at the border and is calculated with the typical convective element velocities of
MLT
,
r_{0}
is the position of the convective border,
f_{\mathrm{ov}}
is the parameter used to calibrate the mixing efficiency
(
Herwig et al. 1997
)
. This approach is based on hydrodynamical simulations by
Freytag et al. 1996
.

A third approach assumes an overshooting which does not affect the thermal structure (i.e. the layer remains radiative), but the chemical elements are mixed instantaneously
(
Pietrinferni et al. 2004
;
Dotter et al. 2008
)
. Even in this case, a parameterised overshooting distance is adopted, as in the first method.

In all methods, the free parameters, e.g.
\lambda_{\mathrm{ov}}
and
f_{\mathrm{ov}}
, must be calibrated on observations.
A good calibration of the internal stellar mixing in stellar models is fundamental because it has implications not only on the stellar properties during the
MS
but also on the post-
MS
evolution
(
Kaiser et al. 2020
;
Johnston 2021
)
.
For this purpose, several works have used different approaches, such as the position of the turn-off in stellar clusters, the stellar properties of wide eclipsing binaries, and asteroseismology measurements
(
Rosenfield et al. 2017
;
Claret and Torres 2019
;
Johnston 2021
, e.g.)
.

Studies on wide binaries have shown a dependence of the overshooting efficiency with the stellar mass. In particular, there is a smooth transition between stars with radiative cores (
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\leq 1\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
) that do not have core overshooting and stars with well-developed convective cores (
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}\geq 1.5\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
).
For higher masses, it is usually assumed that the overshooting efficiency is constant with mass
(
Claret and Torres 2019
;
Costa et al. 2019b
)
.
However, the investigation is still ongoing, and other studies suggest an increasing efficiency of the overshooting for more massive stars
(
Castro et al. 2014
;
Tkachenko et al. 2020
;
Martinet et al. 2021
;
Scott et al. 2021
)
.

New interesting results have been found with asteroseismology, which is the study of stellar interiors through stellar oscillation
(
Aerts 2021
)
.
Asteroseismic measurements allow a better constraining of the stellar internal mixing
(
Aerts 2021
;
Pedersen et al. 2021
;
Johnston 2021
)
, since stellar oscillations are sensitive to the inner properties of stars, such as the angular momentum distribution.
However, there are still not many asteroseismic observations of massive stars, and the most recent studies include stars with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}<25\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}

(
Southworth and Bowman 2022
)
.

On the modelling side, 3-D hydrodynamical simulations are taking several steps forward to improve the treatment of convective boundary mixing in different stellar phases, which could then be included in 1-D codes
(
Scott et al. 2021
, e.g.,)
.
Moreover, 3-D hydrodynamical simulations revealed that convective boundary mixing might excite internal gravity waves, which could propagate and dissipate in the radiative regions transporting angular momentum and contributing to mixing
(
Rogers and McElwaine 2017
;
Aerts 2021
)
.

Theoretically, overshooting should be applied to all convective regions. For instance, many works
(
Alongi et al. 1991
;
Tang et al. 2014
, e.g.)
have been considering the overshoot at the bottom of the convective envelopes of
RGB
stars, which is called the convective envelope overshooting (or undershooting).
Recently, it has been found that undershooting could play a role during
CHeB
phases of metal-poor and very massive stars
(
Costa et al. 2021
;
Volpato et al. 2023
)
.
The undershooting may lead to dredge-up episodes, which extract mass from the helium or carbon-oxygen core, affecting the stellar global stability against the
PI
and, thus, the likelihood of exploding as a
PISN
(Section
III.4
).

For reviews on convection in stellar interiors, we refer to
Salaris and Cassisi 2017
;
Garaud 2018
;
Aerts et al. 2019
;
Ekström 2021
and references therein.

III.2
Mass loss through stellar winds

Stellar winds are one of the key processes in the evolution of massive stars.
They have an impact on the stellar structure, affecting the evolution and determine the final fate of stars
(
Chiosi and Maeder 1986
;
Vanbeveren et al. 1998
;
Langer 2012
;
Meynet et al. 2015
;
Renzo et al. 2017a
, e.g. )
- which depends on their final pre-supernova internal structure configuration
(
Fryer 1999
;
Fryer et al. 2001
;
Heger et al. 2003a
;
O’Connor and Ott 2011
;
Fryer et al. 2012
;
O’Connor and Ott 2013
)
.
Mass loss due to stellar winds not only affects the compact remnants mass spectrum of single stars
(
Belczynski et al. 2010a
;
Fryer et al. 2012
;
Spera et al. 2015
;
Agrawal et al. 2020
)
,
but also plays a fundamental role in shaping the mass spectrum of binary populations that, in turn, affects the predicted populations of merging binary sources of
GW

(
Mapelli et al. 2013
;
Spera and Mapelli 2017
;
Giacobbo et al. 2018
;
Spera et al. 2019
;
Mapelli 2021a
;
Spera et al. 2022
;
Iorio et al. 2023
)
.
Therefore, studying and improving our understanding of stellar winds is fundamental.
Winds also affect the position of the star in the
HR
diagram, therefore, influencing the UV ionizing photon emission of such stars during their life.
Moreover, winds influence the stellar chemical yields, which define how much different types of stars contribute to the metal enrichment of galaxies
(
Goswami et al. 2021
, e.g.)
.

Stellar winds are a very efficient mass loss mechanism for massive stars with initial mass
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}>25\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
. This is related to their very high luminosity.
Usually, stellar winds are not modelled on-the-fly in 1-D stellar evolutionary codes but are included with recipes obtained from observational results or theoretical studies.
In the following, the most common wind treatments are briefly described. For a more comprehensive review of stellar winds of massive stars, we suggest the following references
Puls et al. 2008
;
Vink 2017a
;
Vink 2021
.

The idea that radiation pressure could exert a net force against gravity on the outer layers of stars was established about a century ago by
(
Klotz 1918
;
Saha 1919
)
. But in the late 1960s, with spectroscopic observations, it was discovered by
Morton 1967
that massive stars suffer mass loss driven by line absorption winds. From such observations, they estimated that OB stars might suffer from winds between
10^{-4}
and
10^{-6}\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
/yr, which peel off the H-rich envelope during
MS
.
In the early 1970s, the first line-driven wind theory was developed by
Lucy and Solomon 1970
and later improved by
Castor et al. 1975
.
Within these theories, the rate of mass loss is uniquely determined by the luminosity, mass, and radius of the star.
The CAK theory consists of a method to compute the resulting acceleration from spectral line opacities as a multiplier of the electron scattering acceleration. The force multiplier depends on the local opacity of the matter in which absorption occurs.
The difficult part was to compute all the spectral line contributions to calculate the mass loss rate.
During the 1980s, more and more spectral lines were added to the CAK theory
(
Abbott 1982
;
Kudritzki et al. 1987
)
, and a mass loss metallicity dependence –
\dot{\mathrm{M}}(Z)\propto Z^{\alpha}
, with
0.5<\alpha<0.9
– was established. Stars with lower metallicity retain more mass during their life compared to their high metallicity counterparts and may form more massive
CO
remnants.

Another approach was introduced by
Abbott and Lucy 1985
, which consists of tracking the path and the (multi-)scattering of photons through the stellar atmosphere with Monte Carlo techniques. This method was used by
Vink et al. 2000
;
Vink et al. 2001a
to compute the mass loss of massive OB stars for a wide range of stellar parameters and metallicities.
They derived a mass loss recipe that includes the so-called
bi-stability jump
in
T_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Such a jump depends on the opacity bump resulting from the recombination of Fe IV to Fe III in the stellar atmospheres, at about 20,000 - 25,000 K, which can significantly increase the mass loss.
Furthermore,
Vink et al. 2001a
found that the metallicity exponent (
\alpha
) of the mass loss rate is constant in a wide range of metallicities
1/30\leq Z/{\rm Z}_{\odot}\leq 3
, with
{\rm Z}_{\odot}=0.019

(
Anders and Grevesse 1989
)
.
The general dependence of the mass loss rate on the metallicity is
\dot{M}\propto Z^{0.69}
for
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\geq 25000
K and
\dot{M}\propto Z^{0.64}
for
T_{\mathrm{eff}}\leq 25000
K.
This mass loss recipe is only valid for hot-star winds and is adopted as a standard by many stellar evolutionary codes.
The mass loss estimated by
Vink et al. 2001a
is no longer valid for
RSG
or when the star approaches the Eddington limit.

For
RSG
stars, the mass loss is less constrained than that of hot stars, and stellar modellers usually rely on empirical recipes. The most adopted prescription is given by
de Jager et al. 1988
, which only shows a dependence on the star’s luminosity.
However, this recipe has been shown to overestimate mass loss in post-MS phases, and more updated empirical prescriptions – calibrated on more recent observations – can be found in the literature
(
Beasor et al. 2020
, e.g.,)
.
Some studies suggest that the mass loss in
RSG
could be driven by the radiation pressure on dust, analogously to
AGB
stars
(
van Loon et al. 2005
)
.
There is yet no well-validated theory yet to treat
RSG
winds, recently
Kee et al. 2021
proposed a model in which strong turbulence in the stellar atmosphere may trigger a mass loss able to reproduce the observed data without the inclusion of dust opacities.

When massive stars become very luminous, they can approach the Eddington limit.
Theoretical studies of radiative wind models have shown that stellar winds increase strongly when stars approach the Eddington limit
(
Gräfener and Hamann 2008
;
Vink et al. 2011
)
. This is the case for
LBV
and
WR
.

LBV
are objects that change their spectral type on time scales of years or decades
(
Humphreys and Davidson 1994
)
.
The reason for such changes of spectral type (i.e. in
T_{\mathrm{eff}}
) has not yet been fully understood.
Some studies suggest that it could be related to the envelope inflation caused by the proximity to the Eddington limit
(
Gräfener et al. 2012
;
Vink 2017a
;
Kee et al. 2021
;
Agrawal et al. 2022a
)
.
Recently,
Grassitelli et al. 2021
studied stellar wind mass loss feedback on the envelope structure in
LBV
stars near the Eddington limit. They found that stars perform cycles of hot and cool envelope configurations. Such studies open the door to a consistent theoretical treatment of the
LBV
phase in stellar evolution.

If the stellar winds are strong enough to remove large amounts of the envelope, the star may become a
WR

(
Conti 1975
;
Abbott and Conti 1987
;
Maeder and Conti 1994
;
Crowther 2007
;
Aadland et al. 2022
, Conti scenario,)
.

WR
experience a high mass loss due to their high luminosity and to the proximity of the Fe-opacity bump (at a temperature of about 10
5
K) near the surface.
When stellar winds are very effective, the H-rich envelope could be completely peeled off, and the star becomes a naked pure He star.
For
WR
stars, the empirical mass loss prescription of
Nugis and Lamers 2000
is widely adopted in stellar evolutionary codes.
Recently, new physically consistent models of stellar winds have been computed by
Sander and Vink 2020
. They found a complex luminosity-to-mass and Z-dependent breakdown of WR-type, predicting weaker mass loss winds for
WR
and pure-He stars of few
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
than older mass loss prescriptions.

In the case of very low metallicity, line-driven stellar winds are suppressed. Thus, it is usually assumed that Pop III very massive stars arrive at the final pre-supernova stage with about the initial mass,
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}
.
However, it is known that very massive stars suffer possible mass loss driven by radial pulsations caused by nuclear reactions (the so-called
\epsilon
-mechanism) or by opacity (
\kappa
-mechanism). For more details, see
Baraffe et al. 2001
.
In recent work,
Nakauchi et al. 2020
and later
Volpato et al. 2023
investigate the effect of pulsation driven winds on primordial stars, finding that the pulsation-driven mass loss could be substantial even for zero-metallicity stars.

Although there have been several recent improvements in stellar wind models – thanks to improved methodologies and better calibration on new observations – there are still uncertainties of a factor of 2 - 3 in mass loss recipes
(
Vink 2021
)
, and the scaling with metallicity remains still debated
(
Gormaz-Matamala et al. 2022
;
Marcolino et al. 2022
, e.g.)
.
These uncertainties are mainly due to wind clumpiness (i.e. the homogeneity of winds), the treatment of radiative transfer, and wind hydrodynamics in stellar atmospheres
(
Vink 2021
, see)
.
Such uncertainties may completely change the outcome of massive stars’ evolution with
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}>60\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
, which may end as
BH
or be completely peeled off, ending as
NS
.

From the stellar modellers’ point of view, the most widely adopted formalism for treating mass loss is a collection of prescriptions that uses
Vink et al. 2001a
for OB stars,
Nugis and Lamers 2000
for WR stars and
de Jager et al. 1988
for
RSG
and all other stellar types
(
Eldridge et al. 2017
;
Eldridge and Stanway 2022
)
.
Some authors slightly modified the list of prescriptions to include the Eddington factor dependency by
Vink et al. 2011

(
Tang et al. 2014
;
Chen et al. 2015
, e.g.,)
, or more recent
WR
mass loss by
Sander et al. 2019
;
Sander and Vink 2020

(
Costa et al. 2021
;
Higgins et al. 2021
, e.g.,)
.

Figure
4
:
The plot shows the pre-supernova mass (
M_{\mathrm{Pre-SN}}
) vs the initial mass (
{\rm M}_{\rm ZAMS}
) for stars computed with different initial metal content (Z), in different colours, and with two different wind prescriptions. The continuous lines indicate the final masses of
parsec
tracks computed with new recipes for stellar winds
(
Costa et al. 2021
)
. The dashed lines show pre-supernova masses computed with
sse
code
(
Hurley et al. 2000
)
, which adopt older winds prescriptions for OB stars
(
Nieuwenhuijzen and de
Jager 1990
)
, instead of
Vink et al. 2001a
. A similar plot can be found in
Belczynski et al. 2010a
and
Vink 2021
.

Figure
4
shows a comparison between the final pre-supernova masses versus the initial mass of stars computed with the
sse/bse
populations-synthesis code
(
Hurley et al. 2000
)
which adopts the
Nieuwenhuijzen and de
Jager 1990
winds prescriptions for OB stars, and the
parsec
stellar evolutionary code
(see
Chen et al. 2015
;
Costa et al. 2021
, and references therein)
which adopts prescriptions by
Vink et al. 2001a
for stellar winds.
The figure clearly shows the differences between the two wind prescriptions and how much the predicted pre-supernova mass could change depending on the stellar metallicity. In particular, stars computed with
sse
show an almost unique trend for all stars computed with different initial metallicity. On the other hand, pre-supernova models computed with
parsec
show a clear trend with metallicity.
Stars with a low metal content retain almost all their initial mass, while models with higher metallicity have lower pre-supernova masses.

III.3
Rotation and magnetic fields

III.3.1
Stellar rotation

Rotation is a ubiquitous property of all stars (and objects) in the Universe. It has been found in stars of all spectral types
(
Royer 2009
)
. Interestingly, a dichotomy between low-mass stars and more massive stars has been found. The former are generally slow rotators
(
McQuillan et al. 2014
)
, while the latter show a wide spread of possible rotational velocities and could be fast rotators
(
Hunter et al. 2007
;
Dufton et al. 2013
;
Ramírez-Agudelo et al. 2013
;
Bodensteiner et al. 2020a
;
Schootemeijer et al. 2022
)
.

As mentioned above, rotation is a fundamental parameter affecting the stellar evolutionary path.
It affects stars during all phases and, especially in massive stars, rotation-induced instabilities combined with stellar winds can significantly change their evolution and death
(
Heger et al. 2000a
;
Maeder and Meynet 2000a
;
Hirschi et al. 2004
;
Yoon et al. 2006a
;
Woosley and Heger 2006
;
Georgy et al. 2012
;
Langer 2012
;
Chieffi and Limongi 2013
;
Köhler et al. 2015
;
Choi et al. 2016
;
Ekström 2021
)
.
Stellar lifetimes are generally increased by rotation, from 25% (for a 9
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
star) to 15% (for a 120
\,\rm{M}_{\odot}
star)
(
Maeder 2009
)
.
Rotation impacts how massive stars interact with the environment, affecting the number of ionizing photons emitted and the final yields
(
Limongi and Chieffi 2018
;
Goswami et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
.

Rotation is an inherently 3-D phenomenon, and considering its main effects on the 1-D stellar evolutionary models is not easy. Unfortunately, the adoption of 1-D codes is necessary to follow stars throughout their evolution, from the
ZAMS
to the final phases. Three-dimensional codes are computationally costly, and usually, the simulations are restricted to limited portions of stars and for very short timescales
(
Kuhlen et al. 2003
;
Cristini et al. 2017
;
McNeill and Müller 2022
, e.g.,)
. There are also two-dimensional stellar evolution codes
(
Rieutord et al. 2016
)
, which can simulate the whole star taking into account rotational effects self-consistently, but they are few and slower than 1-D codes. Hence, there is still a lack of big sets of 2-D models which span the different stellar phases.

During the last century, much effort has been made to improve our theoretical understanding of rotating stars. Several pioneering works have opened the path to 1-D modelling of rotation
(
von Zeipel 1924
;
Eddington 1925a
;
Sweet 1950a
;
Strittmatter 1969
;
Kippenhahn and Thomas 1970
;
Endal and Sofia 1976
;
Tassoul 1978
;
Zahn 1992
;
Chaboyer and Zahn 1992
;
Meynet and Maeder 1997
)
.
Of course, this dimensional reduction has some drawbacks. Several approximations must be adopted (such as the
Roche
model to describe the star’s surface), and some parameters (related to the rotational mixing efficiency) must be calibrated to produce stellar models capable of nicely reproducing observations.
In the following, we briefly review how rotation affects the evolution of stars. We refer the reader to the enlightening book by
Maeder 2009
for a more detailed overview of stellar rotation.

Rotation induces two main effects that influence the star.
The first one is the reduced gravity at any point of the star (except on the rotation axis) due to the centrifugal force.
This changes the shape of the star, which is no longer spherical but oblate, with a polar radius smaller than the equatorial one.
In rotating stars, the stellar surface is no longer defined by an equipotential surface because the rotational potential must be considered.
The Roche methodology is usually adopted to describe the stellar surface, at least as a first-level approximation
(
Kippenhahn and Thomas 1970
;
Endal and Sofia 1976
)
.
Using spherical coordinates and assuming the symmetry along the rotation axes, the stellar surface can be described as follows:

-\frac{GM}{R(\theta)}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{2}R(\theta)^{2}\sin^{2}\theta=-\frac{GM}{R_{\mathrm{pole}}},

(III.6)

where
M
is the mass of the star,
R(\theta)
is the surface radius as a function of the co-latitude
\theta
, which is 0 at the pole and 90 at the equator,
\Omega
is the angular velocity (assuming a constant rotation along the co-latitude), and
R_{\mathrm{pole}}
is the polar radius. This equality comes from the assumption that rotation does not modify the polar radius. More detailed models predict a decrease in the polar radius as the rotation increases
(
Sackmann 1970
;
Ekström et al. 2008
;
Deupree 2011
)
.
The rotation rate is a convenient quantity to describe the amount of rotation, and it is defined as:

\omega=\frac{\Omega}{\Omega_{\mathrm{crit}}},

(III.7)

where
\Omega_{\mathrm{crit}}=(2/3)^{3/2}\sqrt{GM/R^{3}_{\mathrm{pole}}}
is the critical break-up velocity.
By rearranging and solving Eq.
III.6
, it can be found that the stellar shape is just a function of the rotation rate
\omega
, parameter, which can go from 0, in case of no rotation, to 1, in case of break-up velocity.
When the star reaches the critical velocity, the centrifugal forces balance gravity, the outer layers become unbound, and the latter can be lost through mechanical mass loss
(
Georgy et al. 2013
, see )
.
To include the geometrical distortion caused by rotation in 1-D stellar evolutionary codes, the
shellular rotation law
is adopted
Meynet and Maeder 1997
.
This assumption is valid when the horizontal turbulence is stronger than the radial one and homogenizes chemical composition and velocity gradients along isobars
(
Zahn 1992
;
Meynet and Maeder 1997
)
.
In practice, the equations of the stellar structure are modified, and two form factors, namely
f_{T}
and
f_{P}
, are included in the momentum equation and energy transport equation. These factors are a function of the rotation rate
\omega
and depend on the shape, surface, and volume of the stellar shell (which are computed from Eq.
III.6
). For more details on the methodologies adopted to include rotation in stellar evolutionary codes, we refer the readers to
Meynet and Maeder 1997
;
Maeder 2009
;
Ekström 2021
and references therein.

The departure from the spherical shape leads to the so-called
Von Zeipel effect

(
von Zeipel 1924
;
Chandrasekhar 1933
)
. Von Zeipel has shown that in a rotating star, the local surface brightness is proportional to the local effective gravity. This drives the effective temperature to be no longer constant along the stellar surface.
Rotating stars are hotter at the poles and colder at the equator.
This effect is also known as
gravity darkening

(
Maeder 1999
;
Aufdenberg et al. 2006
;
Lovekin et al. 2006
;
Espinosa Lara and Rieutord 2011
;
Espinosa Lara and Rieutord 2013
;
Georgy et al. 2014
;
Claret 2016
;
Girardi et al. 2019
;
Abdul-Masih 2023
)
.
The dependence of the effective temperature on the co-latitude (
\theta
) could be expressed as
T_{\mathrm{eff}}(\theta)\propto g_{\mathrm{eff}}(\omega,\theta)^{\beta}
, where
\beta=1/4
is the Von Zeipel parameter.
Several studies predicted lower values for this parameter
(
Lucy 1967
, e.g., 0.08 from)
. Recently, a different analytical description of gravity darkening has been given by
Espinosa Lara and Rieutord 2011
, in which the dependence on the
\beta
parameter is removed. Interferometric observations of rapidly rotating stars by
Domiciano de Souza et al. 2014
found good agreement with the theoretical description by
Espinosa Lara and Rieutord 2011
.

Rotation also enhances the mass loss rates by stellar winds. This is due to the reduced effective gravity along the stellar surface.
Usually, the mass loss enhancement is included in stellar models by including a multiplicative factor (or function) to the adopted wind mass loss prescription, which depends on the rotation rate
\omega
. One of the most popular prescriptions is given by
Friend and Abbott 1986
, which comes from fits on numerical results and reads:

\dot{M}(\omega)=\dot{M}(\omega=0)\left(1-\frac{v}{v_{crit}}\right)^{\xi},

(III.8)

where
\xi
is generally assumed to be
0.43

(
Bjorkman and Cassinelli 1993
;
Langer 1998
)
, and
v_{crit}
is the tangential equatorial break-up velocity, defined as
v_{crit}^{2}=GM(1-\Gamma_{e})/R
, and
\Gamma_{e}=L/L_{E}
is the Eddington factor. This prescription is adopted by several stellar evolution codes that treat rotation
(
Heger et al. 2000a
;
Brott et al. 2011a
;
Chieffi and Limongi 2013
;
Paxton et al. 2013
;
Costa et al. 2019a
, e.g.,)
.
However, this formulation does not include the effect of gravity darkening, namely the dependence of
\Gamma_{e}
on the rotation rate and co-latitude.
More detailed treatments that consider this effect predict anisotropic winds stronger at the poles than at the equator
(
Maeder 1999
;
Maeder and Meynet 2000a
;
Georgy et al. 2011
)
.

The second main effect induced by rotation is an extra mixing that acts in the stars’ radiative regions.
Several mixing processes are induced by rotation
(
Heger et al. 2000a
;
Maeder 2009
, see)
, but the main two are meridional circulation and shear mixing.
The first process is related to the Von Zeipel effect: the thermal imbalance between the poles and the equatorial regions induces large-scale currents that transport material and angular momentum within the star
(
Eddington 1925a
;
Sweet 1950a
, also known as
Eddington-Sweet circulation
,)
.
The second process (shear mixing) occurs if the star has a non-flat rotation profile (i.e. does not rotate as a solid body), and it is due to the friction between two nearby shells, which rotate with different angular velocities
(
Zahn 1974
, e.g.)
.
These two processes induce extra-mixing in the radiative regions of stars, transporting processed nuclear products (like nitrogen) from the stellar core up to the envelope and fresh, unprocessed material from the envelope into the core.
The extra-mixing enhances the stellar lifetimes, makes the star more luminous and enriches the stellar surface with non-pristine chemical elements.

Zahn 1992
and later
Maeder and Zahn 1998
showed that the transport of angular momentum through such processes could be described in a 1-D framework using an advective-diffusive equation.
The radial component of the meridional circulation contributes to the advective transport, while the shear friction contributes to the diffusive one.

Chaboyer and Zahn 1992
showed that the transport of chemical elements could be treated with a diffusion equation, in which the meridional circulation is also included as a diffusive process.
Several evolutionary codes include rotation-induced mixing with the scheme described above
(
Palacios et al. 2003
;
Eggenberger et al. 2008
;
Potter et al. 2012a
;
Chieffi and Limongi 2013
;
Marques et al. 2013
)
.
However, it has been shown that suitable matches with observations could also be obtained with the much simpler diffusive approach to treat angular momentum transport
(
Heger et al. 2000a
;
Brott et al. 2011a
;
Paxton et al. 2013
;
Costa et al. 2019b
)
.

The actual rotation implementation in stellar models requires adopting two parameters to regulate the rotational mixing efficiency,
f_{c}
and
f_{\mu}
. The first parameter directly multiplies the diffusion coefficient in the chemical diffusion equation. The second parameter multiplies the mean molecular weight gradient, which tends to inhibit the transport and has a stabilizing effect.
Since the direct measurements of projected rotational velocities (
v\sin i
, where
i
is the inclination angle between the stellar rotation axes and the line of sight) give only hints of the surface rotational velocities, other observable must be used to probe the internal rotational mixing efficiency.
A typical methodology to calibrate the rotational mixing efficiency is to compare models with data obtained with the VLT-FLAMES survey, which provided the nitrogen surface abundances, and projected rotational velocities for a large sample of massive stars
(
Evans et al. 2005
;
Evans et al. 2006
;
Hunter et al. 2007
)
. Assuming that rotational mixing is the primary process that drives the N-enrichment of the stellar surface, the comparison could be a good tracer of the mixing efficiency.
This calibration is adopted by several stellar codes that treat rotation
(
Hunter et al. 2007
;
Brott et al. 2011a
;
Langer 2012
;
Chieffi and Limongi 2013
, see)
.
However, other processes could compete with rotational mixing (like overshooting, internal gravity waves, and magnetic fields). Therefore, the calibration of the rotational mixing efficiency remains an open problem
(see
Ekström 2021
, and references therein)
.
Asteroseismology promises to improve our understanding of stellar interiors since it can trace the angular momentum transport in the stars and give information on their rotation profiles
(
Aerts et al. 2019
;
Pedersen 2022
;
Aerts 2021
;
Kurtz 2022
)
.
Unfortunately, not many massive stars are analyzed with asteroseismology techniques, but new observations with the TESS space mission are coming
(
Kurtz 2022
)
.

Figure
5
shows examples of massive stars’ evolution with different initial rotation rates (
\omega
).
In the
HR
diagram, at the
ZAMS
, rotating stars look redder and slightly less luminous than the non-rotating ones. In this phase, only geometrical distortion is at play.
Later, during
MS
, the mixing processes begin to provide fresh fuel to the core and extract the processed material.
The stellar surface starts to be enriched by nitrogen. The amount of enrichment depends on the rotation rate.
Rotating stars live longer in the
MS
and, at the end of the H-burning, they are more luminous and have bigger helium cores
(
Maeder and Meynet 2000a
;
Ekström et al. 2012
;
Choi et al. 2016
)
.
After
MS
, the envelope expands and the surface angular velocity drops (due to angular momentum conservation).
The first dredge-up causes a very fast increase in the nitrogen surface abundance.
Nonetheless, the enrichment due to rotation is still appreciable.
During
CHeB
, rotation may also affect the occurrence of the blue loops.

It is important to note that the gravity darkening influences the star’s position in the
HR
diagram and the colour-magnitude diagram, depending on the inclination angle,
i
, between the stellar rotation axes and the line of sight.
Ignoring rotation and gravity darkening could lead to systematic errors in many properties inferred from data-model comparisons, such as cluster age estimates
(
Brandt and Huang 2015a
;
Brandt and Huang 2015b
;
Gossage et al. 2018
)
, and overestimation of the mixing needed to reproduce the luminosity of evolved stars
(
Costa et al. 2019b
)
.

Figure
5
:
The figure shows the properties of tracks with
14\penalty\ \,\rm{M}_{\odot}
and
Z=0.014
with different initial rotation rates (
\omega
). Panel (a): the
HR
diagram. The blue circles (crosses) indicate the beginning (end) of the
MS
phase. The red circles (crosses) indicate the beginning (end) of the
CHeB
phase. Panel (b): convective core in mass fraction versus time. Panel (c): evolution of the surface nitrogen abundances. Markers indicate the different phases as in panel (a). All the tracks are taken from the online public repository
http://stev.oapd.inaf.it/PARSEC
, and a detailed description is given by
Nguyen et al. 2022
.

As anticipated above, rotation may strongly affect the final phases of stars.
Mass loss enhancement in rotating stars leads to smaller pre-supernovae masses than the non-rotating counterparts, possibly leading to smaller remnant masses.
However, rotating stars build bigger He- and CO-cores than non-rotating stars (which affects the explosion likelihood), therefore the final result is not easy to infer
(
Limongi and Chieffi 2018
;
Groh et al. 2019
;
Marchant and Moriya 2020
;
Mapelli et al. 2020a
;
Woosley and Heger 2021a
)
. More details on the final fates are given in Section
III.4
).

In the case of a high rotational mixing efficiency, a very peculiar evolution is predicted for fast-rotating stellar models: the so-called
quasi-chemically homogeneous evolution
.
In this type of evolution, the star is almost entirely homogenised and remains more compact, evolving to higher luminosities and becoming bluer
(
Maeder 1987
;
Yoon and Langer 2005
)
.
The quasi-chemically homogeneous evolution could be an important evolutionary scenario for some supernova, gamma-ray burst events or
GW
progenitors
(
Georgy et al. 2012
;
Martins et al. 2013
;
de Mink and Mandel 2016
;
Riley et al. 2021a
, e.g.)
.

III.3.2
Magnetic fields

Magnetic fields may affect stars’ evolution in several ways
(
Heger et al. 2005
;
Maeder and Meynet 2005
;
Walder et al. 2012
;
Petit et al. 2017
;
Groh et al. 2020
;
Takahashi and Langer 2021
)
.
There are two different kinds of magnetic fields included in stellar models
(see
Braithwaite and Spruit 2017
, for a review)
.
The first is a magnetic field created and maintained by some dynamo processes in the internal regions of stars,
e.g. generated by differential rotation
(
Tayler 1973
;
Spruit 2002
, Tayler-Spruit dynamo mechanism,)
.
In this case, magnetic fields contribute to the transport of angular momentum and chemical elements in the radiative part of the star
(
Maeder and Meynet 2003
;
Maeder and Meynet 2004
;
Maeder and Meynet 2005
;
Heger et al. 2005
;
Potter et al. 2012b
)
.
The other kind is the surface magnetic fossil field.
It could be the remnant field of the stars’ formation process, surviving in a stable configuration.
The fossil magnetic fields may contribute to the magnetic braking (an increase of angular momentum loss) and the mass loss quenching
(
Meynet et al. 2011
;
Amard et al. 2016
;
Georgy et al. 2017
;
Petit et al. 2017
;
Keszthelyi et al. 2019
;
Keszthelyi et al. 2022
, due to the coupling between winds and magnetic field, )
.
The magnetic braking leads to the removal of an additional amount of angular momentum.
It is treated as a torque acting at the stellar surface and is described with an expression obtained from the 3-D simulations results by
ud-Doula and Owocki 2002
;
Ud-Doula et al. 2008
.

Petit et al. 2017
and
Georgy et al. 2017
found that massive magnetic stars at solar metallicity could be progenitors of heavy stellar mass black holes and pair-instability supernovae if the magnetic braking process is at play.

How much the magnetic field affects the evolution of the internal angular momentum is still uncertain. Depending on its strength, the magnetic field could force the solid-body rotation, thus suppressing the rotation mixing.
Otherwise, it could lead to quasi-chemically homogeneous evolution.
New constraints on the internal angular momentum transport could be achieved with the combination of observational measurements and detailed models, but the debate is still ongoing.
Results have been obtained by
Fuller et al. 2019a
from the comparison of new models (which include a modified version of the
Tayler-Spruit dynamo
) with asteroseismology measurements of low-mass stellar cores rotation rates in the giant phase and with
WD
spins.
New models by
Fuller and Ma 2019
predict very low spins for
BH
due to the efficient dissipation of the angular momentum acting during the evolution of the progenitor stars.
However,
Eggenberger et al. 2019
